S2 study · 2026-11-14T07:06:10-05:00

Editing meeting notes with function calls

Current findings and progress

  • The full comparison covers 105 questions and has saved 1,537 of 2,100 planned answers. Details
  • All 1,260 answers are saved and scored; 84 answers have targeted source review, with raw automatic labels retained. Details
  • Native calls reduce mean request-time thinking tokens by 7.5%, while fully supported accuracy changes by -6.2 percentage points. Details
  • Text and audio S2 use separate GPU branches, with at most two A100 80 GB GPUs active. Details

Project checklist · Daily log · Earlier 105-question scores · Audio S2 plan and progress

2. What each model receives and produces

At each existing 25–40 second transcript boundary, the Instruct note writer receives the new speaker-attributed speech, ten seconds of preceding context, one current note state and short receipts from the preceding edits. It sees neither the future question nor its reference answer. Both edit conditions decode freely with the same function descriptions, fields, operations and output cap.

The writer produces JSON operations or native Qwen function calls. The program validates each operation, executes accepted edits and assigns stable note IDs. The next update receives the changed state and receipts; previous snapshots are not accumulated. The current view is limited to 4,096 tokens and contains notes, eight recent changes and original transcript evidence. The full version history is retained on disk.

Table 3. Matched answer conditions; every final thinker retains the full available transcript and receives the same focused answer prompt.
ConditionSaved materialPurpose
ReactiveNone.Baseline without earlier note generation.
Current source notesSelected original utterances and revision links.Reference method from the completed study.
Editable notes · JSONOne current note state, changed through JSON operations.Measure explicit editing with compact factual notes.
Editable notes · native callsThe same note format and operations, using the checkpoint’s native function-call template.Measure the effect of the function-call interface.

The common writer is Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507. Final thinkers are the pinned Instruct and Thinking variants of Qwen3-30B-A3B-2507. Thinking reports native request-time reasoning tokens; Instruct has no separate thinking channel. Earlier note outputs are counted separately from request-time thinking.

3. Memory operations and changes

Table 4. Functions executed by the same memory store in both edit conditions.
FunctionEffect
add_note(topic, fact, owner, status, source_ids)Create a distinct fact, commitment or unresolved issue; return its note ID.
update_note(note_id, source_ids, optional changed fields)Change the same task/aspect using new speech; retain the earlier version and its evidence.
delete_note(note_id, reason)Remove a duplicate or mistaken note from the current view; retain the deletion record.
link_notes(first_id, second_id, relation)Connect existing notes through a revision, confirmation, explanation, qualification or reference.
no_change()Leave the notes unchanged.

Each note contains a topic, short factual text, owner, decision status and source IDs. Source IDs identify transcript passages; note IDs identify program-maintained notes. Add and update operations require at least one newly arrived source. The program rejects edits that break the function schema, cite unavailable speech, exceed size limits or lack that new evidence. Source words, speaker labels and timestamps are copied by the program. These mechanical checks do not establish that a generated fact or owner is correct.

Illustration: Friday is first proposed for sending the slides; Monday is later agreed. The current note changes to Monday, while the change record retains Friday and both supporting passages. A cancellation changes the plan’s status. Automatic budget eviction is recorded separately from model-requested deletion.

Exact function schemas and writer instructions

Flat arguments and one correction turn

The revised interface puts source IDs beside the other function arguments. If an output has invalid syntax or arguments, the program returns the error immediately and allows one correction generation for that segment. Accepted edits remain applied. Both generations and their complete inputs count toward the earlier token cost. Confirming a deadline should preserve the task owner; quantities and participant-specific access reports remain separate.

Why the first pilot was revised

The original interface produced three malformed outputs and 28 rejected operations across 134 updates. In authored controls it changed an owner when another speaker merely confirmed a deadline, confused posts with people, and skipped file-access changes. These errors blocked full evaluation. The revised pilot adds five controls with different speakers or values.

First pilot scores and actual answers

Preliminary full results

The evaluation repeats 55 evaluator controls. Development scores include the source-reviewed corrections shown below, with original automatic labels retained in the download. Full-bank scores combine the automatic judge with targeted source review of 84 answers for 7 questions; the other questions retain automatic judgments. Development contains six previously inspected questions and uses A100 hardware; primary full-study latency uses isolated H100 requests. Missing judge outputs: 0; outputs awaiting required source review: 0.

Scores, intervals, token counts and final answers

Source-reviewed scorer disagreements

The original evaluator sometimes inferred a required fact that the answer omitted, or accepted an incorrect extra reason alongside a correct one. The table uses source-reviewed corrections; the original labels remain in the download.

Question: Assistant, who's sending our main call and when, and why are we asking Claire to send a separate one?

A is sending the main call for linguists by the end of the week. We're asking Claire to send a separate call for psychology/psycholinguistics because she knows the right email addresses and can make it feel more relevant to that audience, which may increase response rates.

A is the sender; today/tomorrow and end of this week are compatible because the source explicitly says Thursday. The answer gives a source-supported reason for Claire: appropriate addresses, relevance or familiarity with recipients. Engagement is described as a purpose or possibility, not a measured outcome.

Question: Assistant, can you clarify how the functional and conceptual design phases differ in terms of deliverables?

Functional design focuses on identifying the required functions and capabilities of the remote control, while conceptual design addresses how the product will be implemented, including user interaction and the identification of components. Detailed design follows conceptual design and involves integrating components.

The answer distinguishes required functions from user interaction. C supplies that distinction at U00037-U00041, so attributing it to C is supported. Component identification is discussed across both phases (U00043-U00045 and U00170); mentioning it during conceptual design is not an exclusive assignment to that phase.

Functional design defines the required functions and capabilities of the remote control (what it needs to do), while conceptual design defines how the design will be implemented (how it will be done), including identifying product components and user interaction. C stated functional design focuses on 'what functions we need' and conceptual design on 'how people are going to use it'.

Assistant, can you clarify how the Yahoo directory labels are used in the evaluation without affecting the model training?

Reference answer: Directory labels are held out from model training and used to compare the learned assignments with the known directory categories.

  • instruct · Reactive · seed 23: The Yahoo directory labels are used solely for evaluation, not for training. They provide ground-truth categories to assess whether the model's topic distributions (from PLSA or the combined model) can correctly predict if unlabelled pages belong to the same category as labelled ones. The model is trained without using these labels, and the evaluation compares the similarity of aspect distributions between pages to see if they align with the directory categories. Fully supported: yes.
  • instruct · JSON edits · seed 23: The Yahoo directory labels are used solely for evaluation, not in model training. A subset of documents is labeled using the directory, and the model's performance is assessed by comparing predicted topic similarities to the true directory categories. The evaluation measures how well the model's aspect distributions predict shared category membership, using a two-step process: first unsupervised training on both labeled and unlabeled documents, then matching unlabeled documents to labeled ones based on similarity in topic distributions. Fully supported: yes.
  • instruct · Source notes · seed 23: The Yahoo directory labels are used solely for evaluation, not for training. They provide a set of known categories for a subset of web pages (P_L_), which are then used to assess how well the model's topic distributions (from P_L_S_A_ or the combined model) can predict the correct category for unlabelled pages (P_U_) by comparing similarity in topic distributions. Fully supported: yes.
  • instruct · Native calls · seed 23: The Yahoo directory labels are used solely for evaluation, not for training. They provide ground-truth categories to assess how well the model's topic distributions align with human-organized categories. The model is trained using only the web pages and their links, without any label information. During evaluation, the model's inferred topic distributions for unlabelled pages are compared to those of labelled pages using similarity measures (e.g., cosine similarity), and the predicted category is assigned based on the closest match. This ensures the evaluation is independent of the training process. Fully supported: yes.
  • thinking · Reactive · seed 23: Yahoo directory labels are used only for evaluation, not for training. As stated in U00164: 'you will not use the Yahoo directory or the directory in the training step of the model.' The labels provide a gold standard to compare document representations, where the model's similarity between documents is evaluated against the directory's category assignments without influencing model training (U00166, U00210). Fully supported: yes.
  • thinking · JSON edits · seed 23: The Yahoo directory labels are used as ground truth for evaluation only. The model is trained unsupervised without using the directory, and the directory labels are used to evaluate prediction accuracy against true category assignments (IN1005-U00211, IN1005-U00225, IN1005-U00234). Fully supported: yes.
  • thinking · Source notes · seed 23: The Yahoo directory labels are used only for evaluation, not for training. 5. Evaluation and interpretation

The development set contains six previously inspected questions from five meetings, plus 10 authored checks for revised deadlines, distinct tasks, speaker ownership, file visibility and quantities with different meanings. Control cases are excluded from the 105-question scores and meeting token totals. Full evaluation requires development output review and a frozen design.

All 105 existing questions across 26 meetings are retained for the later comparison. Results will separately identify the 18 questions used in earlier note tuning and the 44 questions from other meetings; the full bank is not an unseen test set. Reference answers and scorer prompts are unchanged.

  • Report required-fact accuracy and fully supported accuracy, with invalid judge outputs shown separately.
  • Measure native request-time thinking tokens and actual first-answer/completion time; cold timing uses serial requests on one isolated H100.
  • Count all earlier input/output tokens, function definitions, receipts, rejected edits and unused updates, together with request-time costs.
  • Inspect duplicate facts, missed evidence, incorrect owners, changed decisions and notes dropped by the memory budget.

Useful edits must preserve answer quality and improve request-time work or cost. Function-call syntax alone does not establish a latency benefit. Notes plus transcript lookup is a later, separately gated comparison.

6. What changed when notes were added?

  • Native-call notes helped 7 and harmed 20 Thinking answers across 210 trials after targeted score corrections.
  • One recovered microphone answer accounts for about 60 percent of the net thinking-token saving; the median trial uses 15.5 more tokens.
  • Accepted note edits can overwrite correct evidence, add false certainty or distract the answer from facts still present in the transcript.

All 1,260 saved outputs counted; 12 deliberately selected examples inspected; 84 answers for seven questions source-reviewed. This is targeted diagnosis, not a random manual accuracy estimate. Observed note and answer errors do not establish causality; the follow-up below changes only the saved representation.

Table 7. Paired answer changes after targeted source corrections; Instruct has105 trials and Thinking has210 trials across two seeds. Correct means fully supported.
ThinkerSaved inputBoth correctHelpedHarmedBoth wrong

Where the native-call thinking-token savings come from
Table 8. Each row compares the same question and seed with reactive thinking; negative values mean fewer request-time thinking tokens.
Answer outcomeTrialsMean token changeTotal token change
correct -> correct173+6.4+1,101
correct -> wrong20+62.4+1,248
wrong -> correct7-2,367.9-16,575
wrong -> wrong10-1,166.1-11,661

The mean changes by -123.3 tokens, while the median changes by +15.5 tokens. One microphone question changes from a 16,384-token call with no final answer to a correct 897-token answer; its 15,487-token saving is 59.8% of the net saving. Removing trials where either call reaches the cap leaves a mean change of -49.8 tokens; this is a descriptive sensitivity check.

Memory edits and evidence tracking

Across both interfaces, 67 accepted updates keep exactly the same factual text while replacing all previous source IDs: 56 native-call updates and 11 JSON updates, out of 1,260 accepted updates. This is a structural count, not a count of proven false citations. The examples below show concrete unrelated citations.

The writer performs 1,414 repair calls for 2,554 updates. Of 3,165 rejected operations, 1,138 lack newly arrived evidence and 848 exceed the note text budget; repeated repair failures are included. There are 37 malformed outputs. Valid function syntax alone does not ensure that notes preserve meaning.

Source examples: note errors, useful evidence and scorer corrections

Question: Assistant, can you clarify how the functional and conceptual design phases differ in terms of deliverables?

The reactive answer correctly attributes the functional/conceptual distinction to C. The judge called that attribution unsupported because the short key did not name C; the transcript does. Negative judgments need source verification even when the first judge labels them wrong rather than needs_source.

Thinking · Native calls · seed 23

Reactive answer: Functional design specifies required functions (e.g., what the remote must do), while conceptual design focuses on user interaction and interface (e.g., how users will use it), as explained by C during the meeting.

With notes: Functional design focuses on required functions and specific capabilities (what the remote control needs to do), while conceptual design focuses on how the remote control will be structured and presented, including user interaction (how it will be done).

Original speech
  • ES2006a-U00037 · C: I think it th w we're talking the the functional design is more your
  • ES2006a-U00039 · C: um area of things where you'll be we want to look at what functions we need in the remote control and what what specific things it it has to do but the conceptual design is
  • ES2006a-U00041 · C: um perhaps bigger than that and includes the how people are going to use it and and that kind of thing.

Source review added 60 answers from five questions and corrected 14 additional automatic labels; 84 answers across seven questions now have source review. The other 98 questions retain automatic judgments. This targeted review is not a random estimate of judge reliability. Original labels remain in the download.

Review data and examples

7. Follow-up: keep the speech, remove generated claims

Pilot complete: all 144 answers have source review. Can the selected source speech help without the factual errors introduced by generated note text? This diagnostic reuses the native-call notes from the completed study.

Table 9. The four conditions keep the same full causal transcript, question, answer instructions and output format; only saved_results changes.
ConditionInputWhat it tests
ReactiveFull causal transcript and user question.No saved notes; reproduce the existing baseline.
Current NotesThe same full transcript and question, plus the exact saved native-call notes.Reproduce the original note condition.
Quotes CurrentThe same transcript and question, plus only the original source passages selected by those notes.Remove generated facts, owner/status labels, links and change summaries; keep the same source IDs.
Quotes HistoryThe same transcript and question, plus current source passages and earlier evidence for the same active notes.Recover source links overwritten by updates; use only pre-request edits and a 4,096-token quote budget.

Output: the same brief answer, original evidence IDs and missing information. No question or reference answer is used to select saved speech. The speech-only conditions keep original words, speaker labels and times; they do not generate replacement facts.

  • Done: Source-review every pilot answer using the frozen fact checklists. All 144 source-reviewed by Codex; automatic v13 remains unqualified. This manual gate covers only the pilot.
  • Done: Prepare the four inputs and verify causality, source copying, context limits and reproduced prompt hashes. Seven CPU behavior tests,420 inputs and 210 recorded baseline/current-note prompt hashes pass.
  • Done: Run 12 familiar diagnostic questions with four conditions, both thinkers and two Thinking seeds. 144/144 answers generated and source-reviewed; paired costs, latency uncertainty and actual examples are published.
  • Partly complete: Run the user-authorized full105-question comparison with frozen inputs and explicit scoring status. Full inference is running; automatic labels remain provisional until source checks finish.

CPU checks prepared 420 inputs, verified 210 recorded baseline/current-note prompt hashes, and passed seven behavior tests. Earlier evidence is restored for 73 questions within a 4,096-token quote budget. Mean Thinking input sizes are reactive: 14,100, current notes: 18,263, quotes current: 16,316, quotes history: 17,079 tokens. These are input-size measurements, not model-quality or latency results.

Scoring, cost, success criteria and GPU allocation

The revised automatic judge checked the source but still disagreed with reviewed labels. It remains unqualified. The bounded pilot uses source review of every answer; automatic full-bank scoring is not released.

Charge the same 434,979 existing writer-output tokens once to each note-based production scenario; this diagnostic introduces no new writer, so it tests representation, not cheaper note generation.

A useful candidate preserves accuracy within a predeclared 2 percentage-point margin, reduces mean thinking tokens by at least 15 percent and first-answer time by at least 10 percent, with paired intervals supporting the improvement; these are targets, not observed results.

Report all-case accuracy, mean and median thinking tokens, cap hits, missing answers, actual input/output tokens and cold serial first-answer/completion time; keep correct-in-both and cap-excluded slices secondary.

CPU input preparation used 0 GPUs. Each bounded text job uses one A100 80 GB with weights and 8 GiB KV on GPU 0; the text stages run sequentially. H100 timing remains a later gate. The separate audio lane allows at most two study GPUs overall; no S1, ASR, TTS or DuplexOmni service is shared.

Complete follow-up protocol

8. Audio-input S2 in parallel

  • The text diagnostic has saved 144 of 144 answers; all source-reviewed.
  • The audio branch compares reactive answering with function-call notes under two speaker-identity conditions.
  • Each branch uses one GPU at a time, with at most two 80 GB GPUs active across the study.

Current phase: Both models completed the six-minute runtime and native note-call checks. The paired pilot has saved 16/16 answers across two questions; all have source review.

Qwen3-Omni Thinking and MiniCPM-o 4.5 use native audio input and text output through vLLM. Native answer text, audio-segment evidence and uncertainty; generated speech is disabled.

Table 10. Audio-S2 pilot comparison; the same audio history and spoken question are retained within each speaker-identity condition.
ConditionBefore the requestAt the request
Reactive · audio aloneNo note-generation call.All past audio chunks and the spoken question.
Function notes · audio aloneEach 10–40-second thinking interval updates one current note state using audio.The same past audio and spoken question, plus the current notes.
Reactive · labelled voicesNo note-generation call; past-only labelled voice examples identify speakers.The same audio and spoken question, plus labelled examples.
Function notes · labelled voicesAudio-based note updates receive the same past-only voice identity information.The same audio, question and voice examples, plus the current notes.

Local add, update, delete, link and no-change functions preserve audio references and earlier versions; no oracle transcript is stored in the model-visible memory.

  • Ground-truth transcripts and reference answers are evaluation-only.
  • The user question is supplied as audio, not as its known text.
  • Voice examples precede the relevant cutoff; missing identities remain unknown.
  • No future audio is read and no history is silently trimmed.
  • Model encoder windows may be shorter than the 10–40-second thinking intervals; the note-update boundaries stay fixed.

Speaker scoring: Audio-only identities are resolved from cited speech in evaluation, not by comparing arbitrary predicted labels with AMI letters.

Evaluation, resources and actual input controls
  • Accuracy uses source-reviewed fact checklists and native answer text; no response ASR is needed.
  • Report exposed request-time thinking tokens separately from earlier note-generation tokens; use not exposed when no native reasoning channel is available.
  • Report first-answer/completion time, audio input tokens and all earlier encoding/note cost; match cache conditions between reactive and notes.

The automatic text judge agreed on 142 of 155 required-fact checks and did not qualify. The 12-question text diagnostic uses a frozen protocol requiring manual source review of every output; full scaling remains gated.

GPU services are isolated: text and judge jobs use one A100 80 GB or H100 80 GB with weights and 8 GiB KV cache on GPU 0; both bounded audio pilots use one A100 80 GB, with audio encoder, language model and 8 GiB KV on GPU 0. Speech generation is disabled. MiniCPM retains an unused vision tower to avoid a vLLM initialization error; no image input is supplied. The bounded text pilot also uses A100s after pending H100 requests were cancelled before allocation; absolute timing is compared only within this pilot. Longer-history capacity is not established by the six-minute check. Jobs are sequential within each lane; audio models may continue after a different model’s failure. No old DuplexOmni worker or controller is resumed.

Current branch status and protocol

8.1 Audio pilot results

  • Both models completed all audio and note-function calls for the two early questions.
  • Qwen used more request-time thinking with notes in all four matched comparisons.
  • Labelled voice examples did not establish a consistent identity or accuracy benefit in this pilot.

Audio is replayed offline in causal order. Each condition contains two questions, inserted after 351 and 309 seconds in different meetings. Codex checked all 16 native text answers against past source speech; fully supported requires the requested facts and no material unsupported claim. The speaker conditions repeat the same questions and are correlated. These results establish a working pipeline and identify errors for further development; they are too small for a model ranking.

For Qwen, notes increase mean thinking from 372 to 1,680 tokens with audio alone and from 589 to 1,074 with labelled examples. First-answer time also increases in both conditions. Notes restore the sending deadline and help one file-status answer retain the unresolved access problem, giving three fully supported answers out of four compared with zero out of four reactively. The labelled-voice notes still overstate the shared folder’s availability. MiniCPM omits the sending time or overstates file access across the eight answers; its small timing differences do not establish a useful quality–latency improvement.

What was saved: MiniCPM makes only five add and five update calls across 36 updates, with 26 no-change calls; it misses the key file-status facts. Qwen makes 29 add and 13 update calls, but one saved state changes a reported upload into an agreed availability claim. All operations pass the mechanical checks, illustrating why source correctness needs separate evaluation. Each writer receives one current state and bounded recent changes; previous snapshots are retained on disk.

Speaker identity: Audio-only answers may identify a speaker by a unique past utterance or a matching audio segment. Labelled examples make A–D available but descriptions are still accepted when uniquely resolvable. No model answer actually used an A–D speaker label. Timing evidence: Answer content and citation correctness are distinct: some Qwen timestamps are malformed, inaccurate or outside the cited evidence; these are not verified temporal grounding.

No oracle transcript or known question text entered either model. All question-blind background states completed before any question was supplied; reactive and notes share the exact full audio history and spoken question within each identity condition. Cold prefix/multimodal caches per request; first-answer time includes reading/preprocessing, audio encoding, prompt processing and exposed native thinking. MiniCPM has no exposed thinking channel.

Question-duration label audit and repeated answers

Primary Qwen results repeat all eight answers with waveform-derived question durations; the original question-blind notes are reused unchanged. The earlier eight answers displayed a zero-second text label while receiving the complete waveform; they remain archived. CPU tests verify identical audio and prompts differing only in that duration label. Sampling and runtime variability prevent attributing output differences solely to the label correction.

Reviewed audio-pilot outputs, evidence, costs and paired changes

9. Source-quote diagnostic results

  • Source quotes improve several previously failing answers in this selected twelve-question pilot.
  • Thinking uses fewer request-time tokens with source quotes, while the small sample leaves the latency change uncertain.
  • Earlier note generation still dominates the complete generated-token cost.

Both thinkers retain the full transcript. The three saved-material conditions use the same original note writer: current generated notes, the source passages selected by those notes, or those passages plus earlier evidence for the same active notes. No new writer is trained or generated for this comparison.

Evaluation: Codex checked all 144 final outputs against past source speech and the frozen fact checklists. Fully supported means that the answer supplies the requested facts and contains no material unsupported claim. The automatic judge did not qualify and supplies none of these labels. This is a selected development set of 12 questions from eight meetings, with one Instruct seed and two Thinking seeds; it does not replace the 105-question results.

Assistant, can you clarify how the functional and conceptual design phases differ in terms of deliverables?

Functional design specifies required functions; conceptual design concerns use and interaction. Component identification can begin earlier and continue later. Paraphrased quotations and inaccurate evidence IDs are recorded separately from material answer correctness.

Execution, cost and evaluation details

Each allocated job uses one A100 SXM4 80 GB, with its model, audio encoder where needed, and 8 GiB KV cache on GPU 0. Model services are private to each job, and speech synthesis is disabled. Text generation/scoring stages run sequentially, as do audio stages; the study uses at most two GPUs at once.

Earlier note work is counted once per meeting and speaker condition, including unused updates and failed operations. The original text writer is charged to every saved-material condition; extracting source quotes adds no model generation. Native request-time thinking, ordinary output and earlier generation are reported separately. Instruct and MiniCPM expose no separate native thinking channel.

Audio is replayed offline in causal order, and final notes are selected only from boundaries before each question. This study measures the effect of saved content; a later real-time test can measure notes still pending when a request arrives.

Current allocated GPU-hours, including checks and stopped attempts: 5.531. Active GPUs: 1.

The current text and audio branches use separate one-GPU jobs, with at most two 80 GB GPUs active. Model weights, audio encoders where needed and KV cache share GPU 0 within each job; no model service is shared across lanes. Controlled text timing resets the prefix cache before each request.
